To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 1 imprecise p value out of 64 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

P-Value Text
ps > .05 As expected, the Control condition showed no evidence of entrainment at this frequency (both ps > .05), whereas both structured conditions (Frequency and TP) showed reliable entrainment (all ps < .001; see Supplementary Table 2 and Supplementary Fig. 1).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

To improve (5)

To improve Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 33 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Code Comments

Best programming practice is to add comments to code, to explain what the code does (to yourself in the future, or peers who want to re-use your code).

Missing Files

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. The following absolute file paths were found in 1 code file. However, these may be false positives in code like paste0(dir, '/file.csv').

File name Absolute paths found
Block2.m A:_Science_data_output

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In all code files, libraries/imports were loaded in one block.

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 13 distinct packages/libraries: broom, car, data.table, dplyr, emmeans, ggplot2, ggpubr, imputeTS, lme4, lmerTest, patchwork, roll, Smisc. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing an renv.lock (via the renv package), a sessionInfo() text dump, or a groundhog/checkpoint date-pin alongside the code.

All R-type code files (.R, .Rmd, .qmd) could be read in. There were no parsing issues.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 103 columns across 7 data files.

Issues Identified

1 file/column combination has at least one issue.

File Column Issues
eeg_data.txt V6 ⬜ Empty column — Column is empty: all 2808 values are missing

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 7 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A file may have been renamed.

  • EEG_Attention_Correlations.R reads zone_data.txt; your deposit holds zone_data.xlsx.

Check whether they are the same file. If so, use one name in both the script and the deposit.

A package could not be installed.

  • SAT_attentional_zones.R needs Smisc.

Smisc could not be installed with the current version of R, probably because it is old. Record the versions you used (in R, renv::init() writes them to a renv.lock file) and your R version. To be sure the code keeps running, provide a Docker image: a frozen copy of the whole computer setup the code needs, which you can build from rocker/r-ver at your R version.

> sorting_TP <- read.csv("../../../Data/Data_for_R/Behavioral Data/Syllable Sorting Tasks/sorting_TP.csv")
> TP_accuracy <- function(data){
>   tp_pairs <- data %>%
>     arrange(sbj, word_ID, expected_position) %>%
>     group_by(sbj, word_ID) %>%
>     mutate(
>       next_expected = lead(expected_position),
>       next_observed = lead(observed_position),
>       next_box = lead(box)
>     ) %>%
>     filter(!is.na(next_expected), next_expected - expected_position == 1) %>%
>     mutate(
>       same_box = box == next_box,
>       correct_relative_position = (next_observed - observed_position) == 1,
>       tp_correct = same_box & correct_relative_position
>     )
>   
>   tp_pairs %>%
>     group_by(sbj, word_ID) %>%
>     summarise(TP_accuracy = mean(tp_correct, na.rm = TRUE), .groups = 'drop') %>%
>     group_by(sbj) %>%
>     summarise(mean_TP_accuracy = mean(TP_accuracy, na.rm = TRUE), .groups='drop')
> }
> TP_by_sbj <- TP_accuracy(sorting_TP)
> simulate_random_data <- function(n_subj, n_words = 4, n_syllables = 4) {
>

>     
>     syllables <- expand.grid(

MATLAB code, no reproducible output

67 MATLAB code files are present, but this module has no way to extract statistics from MATLAB output, and no self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, a Stata output log, or Mplus output) was found to fall back on. Readers without a MATLAB license cannot currently see what the code produced. Consider depositing the results as well, so they are visible without MATLAB installed: run diary('output.txt') (or diary on) before the analysis and diary off after to save a plain-text transcript of everything printed to the Command Window, or use publish('script.m', 'pdf') (or the Live Editor’s Export/Save As) to generate a single readable document with the code, its output, and any figures together.

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

A total of 78 participants were exposed to statistical regularities embedded within continuous streams of syllables, presented visually at a fixed rate of 4 Hz.

Throughout exposure, participants were asked to complete a color-categorization task that was not related to the statistical structures, but was essential for our measurement of ongoing attentional fluctuations (Decker, Dubois, et al., 2023; Decker, Duncan, & Finn, 2023).

Eighty-seven participants took part in the study (59 women; mean age = 19.43 years, SD = 1.66).

All provided written informed consent and were compensated with $40 CAD or course credit.

Participants completed three conditions (Control, Frequency, and TP) in a counterbalanced within-subjects design.

After exposure to each of the two structured conditions, participants completed a Familiarity Judgment task followed by a Syllable Sorting task (task order fixed).

Participants were informed that the stream “may or may not contain a rule,” and no information about the presence or nature of a pattern was provided before the Syllable Sorting task.

In each condition, participants were exposed to four syllable streams (i.e., blocks), interrupted by self-paced breaks.

Thus, the main logic behind our manipulation is that, if participants were sensitive to the statistical structure of the streams, neural synchronization to the periodic statistical structure should emerge at 1 Hz in the structured streams, reflecting perceptual detection of statistical patterns, and not in the Control condition.

In each stream, 128 of the 960 syllables were colored instead of being black (i.e., in each block, 113 items appeared in red, and 15 appeared in blue), and participants were instructed to press one key (Z) when a red syllable appeared and another key (M) when a blue syllable appeared.

After exposure to each structured condition (Frequency, TP), participants completed a Familiarity Judgment Task, where they rated four-syllable strings (displayed simultaneously as a single unit; e.g., za-si-ro-bu) on a 4-point Likert scale based on whether they followed the same presentation order seen during the exposure phase.

Participants were not informed of the rule but were encouraged to make their best judgment.

Following the Familiarity Judgment Task for the structured conditions, participants were informed about the statistical rule used to arrange the syllables in the exposure stream and were asked to perform a Syllable Sorting Task.

In the Frequency condition, 1 Hz 1 2 time (s) za si ro bu pa ku vi fu ci za za pa bu ci ku ku lo si ci ci ro pa bu bu jo ku ha TP Frequency Control a Trial Number Smoothed RT Deviance (ms) 0 50 100 0 100 50 150 b za �si bu pa ku Press Z Press M Press Z �ro out-zone in-zone participants were presented with all 16 syllables from the exposure stream in a random arrangement at the center of the screen.

For both tasks, participants were given unlimited time but were required to sort every syllable before moving forward with the experiment.

Participants were individually tested in a dimly lit room and positioned in front of a ZOWIE XL LCD Windows monitor with a screen resolution of 1920 × 1080 pixels, and a 60 Hz refresh rate.

To measure individual differences in sustained attention, we computed lapse rates as the proportion of trials in which participants were in a poor attentional state (i.e., out-of-zone), following previous work (Debettencourt et al., 2018; Decker, Duncan, & Finn, 2023; Esterman et al., 2013; Fortenbaugh et al., 2018).

We investigated whether participants were able to group syllables solely on the basis of their absolute position within a sequence.

Ethics approval statement:

All procedures were approved by the University of Toronto Research Ethics Board.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

Field Preregistration 1
template_name OSF Preregistration
title The role of Attentional Fluctuations in the Detection and Recall of Statistical Regularities
id pdmvz
link https://osf.io/pdmvz
date_created 2025-02-26T18:18:17.130865
date_modified 2026-11-06T08:14:17.782890
date_registered 2025-02-26T18:18:17.104111
embargo_end_date
ia_url https://archive.org/details/osf-registrations-pdmvz-v1
research_questions Directional:
Question 1. Does the presence of statistical regularities influence sustained attention? Hypothesis 1. We hypothesize that regularities may draw participants’ attention, leading to differences in sustained attention metrics (i.e., the percentage of time spent in a poor attentional state) between the control group and the two experimental conditions (frequency vs. TP). Specifically, we hypothesize that participants would exhibit fewer attentional lapses when statistical regularities are present compared to the control condition, suggesting increased attention.
Question 2. Do fluctuations in sustained attention affect our ability to detect statistical regularities, as reflected by neural entrainment in the brain? Hypotheses 2. We will use correlational analyses to examine whether neural entrainment at the regularity frequency (1 Hz) is associated with participants’ attentional state (i.e., the percentage of time spent in a poor attentional state). If attention is related to neural entrainment to statistical regularities, we expect a negative correlation between attentional lapse frequency and the strength of neural entrainment.
Question 3. How does sustained attention impact the storage of statistical regularities in memory? Hypothesis 3. If sustained attention influences the storage of statistical regularities in memory, we would expect a correlation between participants’ accuracy on the Familiarity and/or Syllable Sorting Task and the sustained attention metric. The direction of this correlation is uncertain: if sustained attention facilitates the storage of regularities, a negative correlation between lapse rates and behavioral performance would be expected, meaning that as the percentage of time spent in a poor attentional state increases, task accuracy decreases. However, Decker, Dubois, Duncan, and Finn (2022) found that higher rates of attentional lapses correlated with greater learning of task-irrelevant information, suggesting that a positive correlation could indicate better memory storage of statistical regularities when more attentional lapses occur. If no correlation is found between the percentage of lapse rates and task accuracy, this would suggest that attention does not play a significant role in the explicit storage of statistical regularities, which will be discussed in the context of the limitations of null results.
Question 4. How do differences in the strength of neural entrainment for regularities impact the storage of statistical regularities in memory? Hypothesis 4. Prior research indicates that the strength of neural entrainment to statistical patterns correlates with implicit behavioral indicators of statistical learning (see Battering & Paller, 2017). This correlation suggests that, while neural and behavioral measures capture different aspects of learning, both reflect participants’ ability to internalize patterns. However, unpublished data from our lab suggest no correlation between neural entrainment and post-learning behavioral sensitivity to statistical regularities. The present study will therefore extend our knowledge to previously untested conditions. Should a positive correlation emerge, it would indicate that neural entrainment to statistical patterns not only reflects neural pattern detection but also individual differences in memory storage and recall.
Exploratory:
Question 5 and 6. Are different types of statistical patterns processed differently in the brain? Is performance on behavioral statistical learning tasks influenced by the type of statistical patterns? Hypotheses 5 and 6. Statistical regularities based on transitional probabilities have been associated with participants’ ability to store and recall patterns for later use (e.g., Saffran, Aslin, & Newport, 1996). However, to our best knowledge, there is no clear evidence that frequency-based groupings produce the same effect. Moreover, no study has directly investigated whether different statistical patterns (e.g., frequency vs. transitional probabilities) lead to differences in (1) neural entrainment or (2) participants’ ability to recall regularities for post-exposure tasks. Given the limited evidence in literature, we cannot make clear predictions. However, if distinct types of statistical patterns are processed differently in the brain, we anticipate observable differences in the strength of neural entrainment and/or behavioral performance on the Familiarity Task between the frequency and TP conditions.
Question 7. Do more predictable items in the FPVS stream affect participants’ performance and/or attentional state differently from less predictable items? Hypothesis 7. Transitional probabilities (TPs) enable the prediction of which item will appear next in the sequence. In our design, the first syllable of each non-existent word will be less predictable (TP = 0.33) compared to the following three syllables, which will have a high predictability (TP = 1). Given this contrast in predictability, we aim to investigate whether participants’ attentional state or response times vary based on syllable position within the word. If predictability plays a role in modulating attention, we expect to observe differences in attentional lapse metrics and/or response times between the first syllable (less predictable) and the subsequent syllables (more predictable). We also consider the possibility of a graded effect, where attentional lapses/response times progressively change from the first to the last syllable within each word. Specifically, should a difference emerge, we hypothesize fewer attentional lapses/faster response times for predictable rather than unpredictable stimuli. In relation to frequency grouping (i.e., the statistical pattern presented in the frequency condition), base stimuli presented in the first three positions within each second are distinguished from the fourth syllable (oddball stimulus) based on their relative frequency during the stimulation. This frequency pattern is supposed to elicit differences at the level of groups of syllables and not an item-level, thus we do not expect to find significant differences in response times or attentional states between base and oddball syllables. However, if the oddball (deviant) stimuli impact participants’ performance, we might observe differences in the aforementioned measures between base and oddball stimuli.
Question 8. How does neural entrainment differ when participants are in versus out of the zone (in “good” versus “bad” attentional states)? Hypothesis 8. If neural entrainment is enhanced by attention, we hypothesize that it will be stronger when participants are in a good attentional state (i.e., for trials classified as “in-the-zone” compared to when they are in a bad attentional state (i.e., for trials classified as “out-of-the-zone”). If neural entrainment is not altered by attention, then it will be similar regardless of whether participants are in or out of the zone.
study_type Experiment - A researcher randomly assigns treatments to study subjects, this includes field or lab experiments. This is also known as an intervention experiment and includes randomized controlled trials.
blinding For studies that involve human subjects, they will not know the treatment group to which they have been assigned.
study_design_overview This experiment employs a within-subject design with three conditions: control, frequency, and TP conditions. In each condition, participants will complete a Sustained Attention Task. During the frequency and TP conditions, they will additionally perform two tasks: a Familiarity Task and a Syllable Sorting Task.
randomization The order of condition presentation is counterbalanced for each participant. Stimuli are different across conditions (i.e., different groups of syllables are used in each condition) and counterbalanced across participants.
existing_data Registration prior to accessing the data
existing_data_explanation A pilot study with 10 participants was conducted prior to this preregistration to explore the data and evaluate the validity of the paradigm and analysis methods. The pilot data will not be included in the final sample. Non-pilot data collection has started but analyses will not begin until this pre-registration is filed.

None of the analyses outlined in this preregistration have been conducted on our data. All analyses were performed on a separate pilot sample, which did not inform the hypotheses presented here. The pilot data has only been accessed for quality control purposes. Specifically, we aimed to determine whether the modification to the Fast Periodic Visual Stimulation Paradigm still elicited the expected neural entrainment in the brain and whether the planned sustained attention analysis provided a valid measure of attentional processes for this specific paradigm.
data_collection_procedures To be enrolled in the experiment, participants must be fluent English speakers, between 18 and 35 years of age, with normal or corrected-to-normal vision, and with no record of neurological, or language impairment. All genders will be included in the study. In exchange for participation, participants will receive either a monetary reimbursement of 40$ (20$/h) or course credits.
Exclusion Criteria
Participants will be excluded from the analysis if their overall discrimination accuracy in the Sustained Attention Task is less than 75%. We reserve the right to adjust our exclusion criteria based on the final data (e.g., the distribution of the participants’ performance in the Sustained Attention Task turns out to be substantially better, or worse, than expected), but we will clearly justify and document any changes made.
sample_size 78 participants who meet the inclusion criteria will be included in the study.
sample_size_rationale For our power analysis, we focused on the primary research question that requires the highest statistical power: the individual-difference correlation between neural entrainment and behavioral data. Drawing on the only other study (Batterink and Paller, 2017) that examined the relationship between neural entrainment (using a different measure) and behavior (also with a different behavioral measure), we anticipate a small to moderate effect size, with a correlation around 0.33. Using the pwr toolbox in R, we determined that a minimum sample size of 65-75 participants is needed to achieve adequate power. To achieve a full counterbalanced sample, we set the final sample to 78 participants.
stopping_rule We will conclude data collection once we have recruited 78 participants who meet the inclusion criteria for the study and do not fall under any of the exclusion criteria.
manipulated_variables Manipulated variables
Sustained Attention Task: To measure sustained attention, we manipulate the relative frequency of blue and red syllables in the experiment. Ninety percent of the trials feature red syllables (frequent trials), while 10% involve blue syllables (infrequent trials). Since black syllables are interspersed between these trials, it’s important to note that, in this study, a “trial” refers to a colored syllable (i.e., red or blue syllables) requiring a participant’s response.
To gather electrophysiological data on participants’ detection of regularities, we manipulate the presence of statistical patterns in the Fast Periodic Visual Stimulation (FPVS) stream. Specifically, we vary the arrangement of syllables to create either random sequences (control condition) or specific statistical regularities— frequency differences in the frequency condition and transitional probabilities in the TP condition.
Familiarity Task: We manipulated the structure of the syllable strings presented during the task. In the frequency condition, we varied the frequency of the oddball syllables within the string. The levels of correctness were: (1) correct—three base syllables and one oddball, (2) partially wrong—two base syllables and two oddballs, and (3) completely wrong—one base syllable and three oddballs. For the TP condition, we manipulated the structure of the non-word presented to participants. The levels of correctness were: (1) correct—a “word” presented during the FPVS stream, (2) partially wrong—the ending part of one “word” combined with the starting part of another, forming a high transitional probability incorrect string, and (3) completely wrong—a string composed of one syllable from each “word” presented during FPVS, resulting in a low transitional probability string.
Syllable Sorting Task: In this task, no variables are manipulated. Participants will simply be presented with a drag and drop task and asked to recreate the statistical structure that was presented during FPVS by sorting syllables in different boxes.
measured_variables Sustained Attention Task:
Classification accuracy for both frequent and infrequent trials
Response time for each trial
Electrophysiological activity throughout the FPVS stimulation

Familiarity Task:
Ratings (1-4 values on the likert scale for correct, partially wrong and completely wrong strings of syllables)

Syllable Sorting Task (Frequency):
Sorting accuracy

Syllable Sorting Task (TP):
Grouping accuracy
Transitional Probability Accuracy
Position Accuracy
indices 1. Mean classification accuracy • for frequent (red) and infrequent (blue) trials during the Sustained Attention Task for each participant.
Sustained attention analyses:
Building on previous research demonstrating that trial-by-trial fluctuations in RT deviance (Esterman et al., 2014) and speed (DeBettencourt et al., 2018) are linked to variations in attention and task performance, we aim to identify the most suitable index of sustained attention to use in our analyses. Following previous work (Decker, Dubois, Duncan & Finn, 2022) we adopted a data-driven approach to compare how well a moving average of RT speed versus RT deviance predicts trial-by-trial accuracy. Based on pilot data from 10 participants, RT speed appears to be a more sensitive indicator of attentional lapses than RT deviance (see Decker, Dubois, Duncan, Finn, 2022). Therefore, we plan to focus our primary analyses on RT speed and provide details on this index here, while reporting RT deviance results in the supplementary materials for completeness. However, we will verify whether RT speed remains the best predictor when analyzing data from the final sample. If RT speed is no longer the most sensitive indicator of attentional lapses, we will instead use RT deviance as our primary measure.
2. Response times • For each participant, response times will be regressed onto trial number, and residuals will be extracted and used in all analyses. Subsequent references to response time refer to residualized response times. Residual response times will also be mean centered within participants, condition and block.
3. The mean absolute deviation of response time during the Sustained Attention task, for each trial for each participant.
4. Smoothed variance time course
• First, residualized response times for missed and infrequent trials will be interpolated linearly by averaging response time data from the four surrounding trials (two trials before, two after). Since participants are allowed to take breaks between blocks, interpolation is done within each block and condition. In the case that observations from the four surrounding trials are missing, the window size is automatically increased until there are at least two non-missing values.
• Mean absolute RT deviance for each trial (the absolute value of the difference between a trial’s RT and a participant’s mean RT).
• Smoothed absolute RT deviance for each trial: mean absolute RT deviance values are smoothed using a three-trial moving average.
5. Attentional zone
• A categorical variable indicating participants’ attentional state on each trial.
• The median value of each participant’s smoothed variance time course data will be identified, and these values will be averaged across participants to produce a single value.
• This single value will be used as a “cut-off” to assign participants’ response time data on each trial to either a low or high variability zone. Trials whose response time deviates more than this cut-off value will be coded as “poor”, whereas trials that deviate less will be coded as “good”. For analyses based on speed, trials will be coded as “poor” if the moving time course of RT speed exceeds the participant’s mean moving time course and deviates more than the group threshold.
6. Attentional zone percent • Based on the variable attention zone, we will calculate the percentage of total trials that each participant encodes during a good vs. poor attentional state.

Familiarity Task:
7. Mean Ratings • we will calculate the mean ratings for each trial type (correct, partially wrong, completely wrong)

Sorting Task:
8. Sorting Accuracy (for frequency condition) • Accuracy for each trial (i.e., for each of the 16 syllables) will be coded as 1 when oddballs are categorized as ‘infrequent’ and bases as ‘frequent’ in the Syllable Sorting Task.
9. Transitional Probability Accuracy (for the TP condition) • This measure assesses how accurately participants grouped syllables based on their transitional probability (TP), specifically focusing on adjacent syllables where TP = 1. The measure involves three checks: Same Box Check: Determines whether syllables from the same word are placed in the same box (as the aforementioned measure). Relative Position Check: Verifies that syllables in the same box maintain the correct relative order. Adjacency Check: Confirms that the syllables are adjacent in the expected sequence (TP = 1). A pair is marked as TRUE only if all three conditions are met, indicating correct placement based on TP. The final accuracy is calculated as the mean proportion of TRUE scores across all syllable pairs, providing a measure of how well participants grouped adjacent syllables according to their TP.
10. Position Accuracy (for the TP condition) • This measure evaluates the effect of syllable absolute position on participants’ performance. It is calculated by comparing the expected position of each syllable with its observed position.
statistical_tests Question 1. Does the presence of statistical regularities influence sustained attention? We will use mixed-model analyses to assess the effect of condition on attentional lapses. We will run a mixed model analysis to predict attentional lapse scores across the three conditions Example code 1) glmer(zone ~ condition + (1 | sbj), data = dataframe, family = binomial). Additional models will include predictors such as condition, condition order and trial order, possibly with their interactions as well: example code 2) glmer(zone ~ condition + condition_order +/* trial_order + (1 | sbj), data = dataframe, family = binomial).
Question 2. Do fluctuations in sustained attention affect our ability to detect regularities, as reflected by neural entrainment in the brain? For each condition containing statistical regularities (i.e., Frequency and TP conditions), we will examine the correlation between neural entrainment at 1 Hz and attentional lapse scores within the same condition. If the assumptions of Pearson’s correlation are met, we will use this test to quantify the relationship.
Question 3. How does sustained attention impact the storage of statistical regularities in memory?
We will examine the correlation between attentional lapse scores and the indices derived from the familiarity and syllable sorting tasks to assess whether attention influences participants’ ability to explicitly store and recall statistical regularities. If the assumptions of Pearson’s correlation are met, we will use this test; otherwise, we will apply a suitable non-parametric alternative. These correlations will be conducted separately for the TP and Frequency conditions.
Question 4. How do differences in the strength of neural entrainment for regularities impact the storage of statistical regularities in memory? We will compute Pearson correlations between participants’ neural entrainment to statistical regularities and their accuracy indices from all behavioral tasks, provided that all assumptions are met. This analysis will be conducted separately for the TP and Frequency conditions.
Throughout the analyses, we will consider adding random slopes for subjects, to account for subject-specific variability in the overall, fixed effects. We will try them out and see whether the fitting process can sustain it (e.g., no singularity or convergence issues emerge).
inference_criteria We will use p values (two-tailed). An effect will be considered significant if p < 0.05.
data_exclusion_criteria Participants will be excluded from the analysis if their overall discrimination accuracy in the Sustained Attention Task is less than 75%. Additionally, individual trial response times that are exceptionally fast or slow, and thus considered unreliable, will be excluded. As stated before, since cutoff values for response times will be determined based on the response time distribution across all participants, we will not specify them in advance. We reserve the right to adjust our data exclusion criteria based on the final data, but we will clearly justify and document any changes made.
exploratory_analyses Question 5. Are different types of statistical patterns processed differently in the brain? We will use a linear model on electrophysiological data (neural entrainment at 1 Hz) to assess the effect of the three conditions on neural entrainment. Example code 1) lm(data ~ conditionhemisphere, data = eeg_data)
Question 6. Is performance on behavioral statistical learning tasks influenced by the type of statistical patterns? We will run a mixed model analysis on ratings in the familiarity task to compare 1) whether participants correctly distinguish correct from partially wrong and completely wrong sequences 2) participants’ memory for regularities between the two regularities conditions. example code 1) lmer(rating ~ TrialType (correct vs. partially_wrong vs. completely_wrong)condition + (1 | sbj), data = dataframe). Mixed model will help consider the effect of other predictors. Example code 2) lmer(familiarity_acc ~ TrialType (correct vs. partially_wrong vs. completely_wrong) + condition + rep_order + (1 | sbj), data = dataframe)
Question 7. Do more predictable items in the FPVS stream affect participants’ performance and/or attentional state differently from less predictable items?
We will use mixed-model analyses to assess the effect of condition on attentional lapses and RTs. We will run a mixed model analysis to predict attentional lapse scores/RTs across the three conditions Example code 1) glmer(zone ~ conditionposition + (1 | sbj), data = dataframe, family = binomial). Example code 2) 1) lmer(rt ~ conditionposition + (1 | sbj), data = dataframe).
Question 8. How does neural entrainment differ when participants are in versus out of the zone (in “good” versus “bad” attentional states)? Exploratory analysis will be first conducted on the independent sample (n=10) in order to determine how to bin out of the zone and in the zone trails with sufficient temporal signal in order to measure entrainment to both oddball and transitional probability regularities. If this analysis is possible, these processes will then be applied to the full sample.
paper_id input

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of deviations from preregistrations, see:

van den Akker O, Bakker M, van Assen M, Pennington C, Verweij L, Elsherif M, Claesen A, Gaillard S, Yeung S, Frankenberger J, Krautter K, Cockcroft J, Kreuer K, Evans T, Heppel F, Schoch S, Korbmacher M, Yamada Y, Albayrak-Aydemir N, Wicherts J (2024). “The potential of preregistration in psychology: Assessing preregistration producibility and preregistration-study consistency.” Psychological Methods. doi:10.1037/met0000687.

For educational material on how to report deviations from preregistrations, see:

Lakens, Daniël (2024). “When and How to Deviate From a Preregistration.” Collabra: Psychology, 10(1), 117094. doi:10.1525/collabra.117094.
