How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Without even being aware, humans are amazingly sensitive to the structures around them. Is this sensitivity constant or does it fluctuate along with our attentional states? To answer this foundational question, we deployed temporally sensitive measures that allow us to track neural entrainment-the synchronization of brain activity to environmental regularities-alongside fluctuations in attentional states. Participants (n = 78) viewed both random and structured visual streams while performing a task to index fluctuating attentional states. Structured streams evoked robust neural entrainment and reduced attentional lapses. Moreover, attentional states varied with neural entrainment. Across participants, those who displayed greater neural entrainment had reduced attentional lapses. And critically, even within individuals, greater entrainment was observed during periods of greater attentional focus. Our sensitivity to structure and attentional states are therefore inextricably linked: structures stabilize our attentional states and our sensitivity to structure dynamically mirrors the ebb and flow of human attention.

1 Introduction

Humans have a formidable capacity to learn from statistical regularities in the environment. This capacity-commonly referred to as statistical learning-has been proposed as a mechanism underlying a wealth of fundamental cognitive skills, including visual learning (Fiser & Aslin, 2001), word identification (Lelonkiewicz et al., 2023; Saffran et al., 1996), semantic memory (Andrews et al., 2009), and even syntactic processing (Kidd, 2012). Evidence for statistical learning has been documented across development, from the cradle to the grave (Forest et al., 2023), and across species (Conway & Christiansen, 2001; Santolin & Saffran, 2018). Although various questions remain about how statistical learning interfaces with and even differs from other known cognitive processes (Frost et al., 2019), it is unquestionably a widespread learning mechanism, enabling the organization and interpretation of structured information in our environments (Sherman et al., 2020). One pressing question about statistical learning concerns its relationship with attention. Does attention constrain and shape this learning system, like it does for other forms of learning (Decker & Duncan, 2020)? Or does statistical learning log environmental regularities regardless of our attentional goals and states? Knowing the answer is critical for understanding how and when humans learn about the world around them. On the one hand, statistical learning is often described as an implicit phenomenon, which can easily happen outside of attention (Batterink & Paller, 2019; Duncan & Theeuwes, 2020). On the other hand, numerous studies have reported that diverting attention away from structured input can impair statistical learning (Duncan et al., 2025; Toro et al., 2005; Turk-Browne et al., 2005). These divergent findings likely reflect methodological factors. Among them, in previous studies attention is manipulated exogenously-most often by perturbing attentional allocation through demanding concurrent tasks-without directly quantifying attentional engagement to the regularities, such that attention to the input may vary across studies and individuals. Another important point is that this previous work often assesses statistical learning during a post-test after learning has already happened, often using tasks that require explicit judgments or strategic retrieval. Such offline measures are likely to recruit aspects of cognition beyond online statistical learning processes (Batterink et al., 2015; Siegelman et al., 2018) and are subject to idiosyncratic task-strategy effects, cloaking the online dynamics of learning. Most importantly, these approaches preclude direct examination of how naturally occurring fluctuations in sustained attention interact with the moment-by-moment processing of statistical structure.

Here, we address this fundamental gap in our knowledge by tracking statistical learning alongside the natural ups and downs of attention. To this end, we leverage neural entrainment-the synchronization of neural activity to higher-order temporal structure in sensory input (Batterink & Paller, 2017)-as an online index of statistical learning. Because attention naturally fluctuates over time, alternating between periods of focus and inattention (Debettencourt et al., 2018; Esterman et al., 2013; Fortenbaugh et al., 2018), this neural synchronization necessarily operates under dynamically varying attentional states. Importantly, neural entrainment to structure can be observed even when post-learning behavioral measures do not reflect learning (Farthouat et al., 2017; Ruzza & Crepaldi, 2026), and in populations with markedly reduced or absent conscious awareness, including sleeping neonates (Fló et al., 2022), sleeping adults (Batterink & Zhang, 2022), and minimally conscious patients (Benjamin et al., 2025). We do not yet know if neural entrainment simply persists in states of reduced attention, or is actively amplified when attention is fully engaged. If entrainment and attention are related, it would show that our brains are not only passively responsive to the presence or absence of structure; rather, it would suggest that our brains interact with our internal cognitive states to enhance processing when we are "in the zone".

The current preregistered study was designed to reveal the interplay between neural sensitivity to structure and fluctuations in attentional focus. A total of 78 participants were exposed to statistical regularities embedded within continuous streams of syllables, presented visually at a fixed rate of 4 Hz. Across conditions, the visual input was closely matched but differed in statistical structure: streams either contained a regularity in which lower-frequency syllables appeared periodically at 1 Hz among higher-frequency syllables (Frequency condition); a regularity where syllables were grouped into fixed four-syllable units and also occurred at 1 Hz (Transitional Probability, TP, condition); or no statistical structure at all (Control condition). Throughout exposure, participants were asked to complete a color-categorization task that was not related to the statistical structures, but was essential for our measurement of ongoing attentional fluctuations (Decker, Dubois, et al., 2023; Decker, Duncan, & Finn, 2023). Finally, we assessed learning through post-exposure behavioral tasks, which allowed us to evaluate whether neural sensitivity to statistical structure during exposure translated into subsequent behavioral evidence of learning. We tested whether endogenous fluctuations in sustained attention can predict the degree of neural entrainment to statistical regularities. If attention constrains the real-time perception of statistical regularities, then attentional lapses-indexed by increased response time (RT) variability and classified as periods spent out-of-zone-should be associated with reduced neural entrainment to regularities. Alternatively, if neural entrainment reflects processes that are independent of sustained attention, entrainment may be preserved during periods of reduced attentional engagement. To preview, we found a strong relationship between attentional lapses and entrainment, both across and within individuals. These findings show that trial-by-trial fluctuations in attention covary with neural sensitivity to statistical structure during online exposure, indicating that neural entrainment appears to reflect an attention-sensitive index of learning.

2 Methods

2.1 Participants

Eighty-seven participants took part in the study (59 women; mean age = 19.43 years, SD = 1.66). Per preregistered criteria (18-35 years old, right-handed, fluent English speakers with normal or corrected vision and no cognitive, linguistic, or neurological conditions), nine were excluded (two for < 75% accuracy, two left-handed, four reporting ADHD, one autism), yielding a final sample of 78 participants. All provided written informed consent and were compensated with $40 CAD or course credit. All procedures were approved by the University of Toronto Research Ethics Board.

2.2 Stimuli

Forty-eight unique syllables were generated by pairing 16 consonants with one of four vowels (a, i, o, u), such that each consonant appeared once per list and each vowel appeared four times, yielding three balanced lists of 16 syllables. Lists were assigned to experimental conditions and counterbalanced across participants. For visual presentation, each syllable was rendered as a 500 × 150 px image in Courier New font (in black, red, and blue) on a light gray background using a custom R script, ensuring uniform appearance across conditions.

2.3 Experimental paradigm

Participants completed three conditions (Control, Frequency, and TP) in a counterbalanced within-subjects design. In each condition, they first performed a Sustained Attention Task while viewing a continuous syllable stream. The stream contained no regularities in the Control condition but encoded structured patterns in the Frequency and TP conditions. After exposure to each of the two structured conditions, participants completed a Familiarity Judgment task followed by a Syllable Sorting task (task order fixed). Participants were informed that the stream "may or may not contain a rule," and no information about the presence or nature of a pattern was provided before the Syllable Sorting task. Before each new condition, they were told that any possible pattern would differ from earlier ones.

2.3.1 Exposure phase and sustained attention task

In each condition, participants were exposed to four syllable streams (i.e., blocks), interrupted by self-paced breaks. Each block lasted 4 minutes and consisted of the continuous presentation of 960 syllables at a steady rate of 4 Hz (four syllables per second), resulting in a total of 16 minutes of exposure per condition. Visual presentation was achieved through sinusoidal contrast modulation: syllables gradually increased in contrast against a light gray background, reaching maximum contrast (125 ms) and then decreasing back to baseline (250 ms, Fig. 1a).

In the Control condition, syllables were displayed in pseudo-random order (avoiding close repetitions of the same syllable), and each one appeared exactly 60 times (960/16). Crucially, this condition lacked any internal periodicity, apart from the fixed 4 Hz presentation rate of the syllables.

In the Frequency condition, the 16 syllables inside each list were arbitrarily divided into two equally sized groups: frequent (F) and infrequent (I). These groups were uniquely generated for each participant. A statistical regularity was introduced by manipulating the syllables' distribution within a stream: one syllable from the infrequent group was interleaved every three frequent syllables (e.g., F-F-F-I-F-F-F-I). This manipulation created a frequency imbalance: in the absence of any other difference between both groups, and because each group contained an equal number of syllables, each frequent syllable was presented three times more often (90 times) than each syllable assigned to the infrequent group (30 times).

Lastly, in the TP condition, the 16 syllables were arbitrarily grouped into four sets of four-syllable-units (e.g., za-si-ro-bu, pa-ku-vi-fu). These units were uniquely generated for each participant and presented sequentially in a pseudo-random order (i.e., no unit could repeat itself; all units were shown exactly 60 times) during a presentation block. Within each unit, the TP between adjacent syllables (e.g., za-si) was 1.0, while the TP between syllables across unit boundaries (e.g., bu-pa) was 0.33.

In both structured conditions (Frequency, TP), statistical regularities occurred at a frequency of 1 Hz (i.e., every 4 syllables). Thus, the main logic behind our manipulation is that, if participants were sensitive to the statistical structure of the streams, neural synchronization to the periodic statistical structure should emerge at 1 Hz in the structured streams, reflecting perceptual detection of statistical patterns, and not in the Control condition.

To measure fluctuations in sustained attention, participants performed a categorization task during exposure to the syllable streams (Fig. 1b). In each stream, 128 of the 960 syllables were colored instead of being black (i.e., in each block, 113 items appeared in red, and 15 appeared in blue), and participants were instructed to press one key (Z) when a red syllable appeared and another key (M) when a blue syllable appeared. Importantly, any given syllable could appear in black, red, or blue within the same presentation block, as colors were not associated with any syllable. Colored syllables (i.e., trials) were pseudo-randomly distributed throughout each stream, allowing participants adequate time to respond (i.e., at least one second) and ensuring no secondary statistical structure was created.

2.3.2 Familiarity judgment task

After exposure to each structured condition (Frequency, TP), participants completed a Familiarity Judgment Task, where they rated four-syllable strings (displayed simultaneously as a single unit; e.g., za-si-ro-bu) on a 4-point Likert scale based on whether they followed the same presentation order seen during the exposure phase. Participants were not informed of the rule but were encouraged to make their best judgment. Strings were categorized as correct, partially wrong, or completely wrong, with different constructions for each condition. In the Frequency condition, correct strings followed the syllable frequency distribution from the exposure phase (F-F-F-I), partially wrong strings equated the frequency of the two groups (F-F-I-I), and completely wrong strings inverted the frequency pattern (F-I-I-I). In the TP condition, correct strings were the same words presented during exposure (e.g., za-si-ro-bu). Partially wrong strings concatenated syllables from two different words while preserving withinword transitional probabilities (e.g., ro-bu-pa-ku). Completely wrong strings violated all transitional probabilities, with their relative position within the word preserved (e.g., za-ku-ni-ha). Participants evaluated each string type (4 correct, 4 partially wrong, and 4 completely wrong) three times, in a blocked order with no time limit.

2.3.3 Syllable sorting tasks

Following the Familiarity Judgment Task for the structured conditions, participants were informed about the statistical rule used to arrange the syllables in the exposure stream and were asked to perform a Syllable Sorting Task. This task tested their acquired knowledge of the previously presented statistical structure by asking them to consciously retrieve the learned pattern from memory. In the Frequency condition, 1 Hz 1 2 time (s) za si ro bu pa ku vi fu ci za za pa bu ci ku ku lo si ci ci ro pa bu bu jo ku ha TP Frequency Control a Trial Number Smoothed RT Deviance (ms) 0 50 100 0 100 50 150 b za �si bu pa ku Press Z Press M Press Z �ro out-zone in-zone participants were presented with all 16 syllables from the exposure stream in a random arrangement at the center of the screen.

Their task was to sort them into two boxes, labeled frequent and infrequent. No information was provided regarding the number of syllables in each group, forcing participants to rely only on their knowledge of the frequency distribution.

In the TP condition, participants reconstructed the four units by dragging each syllable into one of four boxes and placing it in the correct serial position (1-4). For both tasks, participants were given unlimited time but were required to sort every syllable before moving forward with the experiment.

2.4 Procedure

Participants were individually tested in a dimly lit room and positioned in front of a ZOWIE XL LCD Windows monitor with a screen resolution of 1920 × 1080 pixels, and a 60 Hz refresh rate. Stimuli were presented using PsychToolbox-3 (Brainard, 1997), and were displayed at the center of the screen. The keyboard and mouse were positioned in front of the participants. Instructions for each task were provided both in writing and orally by the experimenter to ensure that participants fully understood the procedures.

2.5 Data Analysis

2.5.1 EEG acquisition and pre-processing

Electroencephalographic (EEG) activity was recorded with BioSemi ActiveTwo system (Biosemi, Amsterdam, Netherlands) at a sampling rate of 1024 Hz. Sixty-four active electrodes were positioned on the scalp following the radial-ABC system using an elastic cap, while electrode offsets were maintained within ±20 mV. Data were acquired unreferenced (BioSemi CMS/DRL). Preprocessing was performed in MAT-LAB (The MathWorks Inc., 2024) through a combination of custom scripts and the EEGLAB toolbox (Delorme & Makeig, 2004). Data were first filtered with a 0.1-100 Hz band-pass filter. We removed breaks between blocks to keep only the signal of interest, and we identified and removed channels exhibiting artifacts by visually inspecting the raw signal waveforms and power spectra. Independent Component Analysis (ICA) was applied to remove ocular artifacts (blinks and saccades). Rejected channels were reconstructed using spherical interpolation. The continuous EEG was then segmented into 240-second epochs and re-referenced offline to the common average. This procedure produced one dataset per participant, block (1-4), and condition (Control, Frequency, TP), with each block lasting 240 seconds.

2.5.2 Frequency domain analysis

Each dataset underwent a Fast Fourier Transform (FFT), from which normalized amplitude spectra were derived for each channel. Normalization involved taking the absolute value of the FFT and dividing it by the number of data points. To reduce noise fluctuations across the frequency spectrum, thus mitigating the influence of boundary effects and outliers, a local baseline correction (BC) was applied. Baseline at each frequency bin was computed as the mean of 80 neighboring bins (40 per side), excluding the three bins immediately adjacent to the target to avoid spectral leakage. To ensure a robust estimate of the local noise floor, we further excluded the local minimum and maximum values within this window before averaging. This mean was then subtracted from the observed amplitude. The bin selection follows the same methodology established in previous studies (De Rosa et al., 2022), adjusted for the finer frequency resolution of this experiment (1/240 = 0.0042 Hz). Responses at our target frequencies were quantified as the combined BC amplitudes respectively at the syllable (4 Hz) and structure (1 Hz) stimulation frequencies, along with their first two harmonics (syllable: 8 Hz and 12 Hz; structure: 2 Hz and 3 Hz).

2.5.3 ROI analysis

We investigated the EEG response to our syllable (4 Hz) and structure (1 Hz) frequencies. First, the response to syllable stimulation frequency (4 Hz) was analyzed within the occipito-parietal region (Norcia et al., 2015) comprising 4 electrodes surrounding and including Oz. Then, for all three conditions we examined the EEG response to the pattern stimulation frequency (1 Hz) within two predefined regions of interest (ROIs). These ROIs were selected based on prior research utilizing visual linguistic stimuli (De Rosa et al., 2022) and adapted for the lower number of overall electrodes. The primary areas of interest for pattern responses included a cluster of six sites in both the left (focal point PO7) and right hemispheres (focal point PO8) and within the occipito-temporal region.

To evaluate whether neural entrainment was above the noise level within each condition, responses were first averaged across channels within each ROI for each participant, yielding four block-wise values, which were then averaged and subjected to one-sample t-tests, in keeping with previous work (De Rosa et al., 2022). For the structure-rate frequency (1 Hz), differences between conditions were assessed using a linear mixed-effects model in R (R Core Team, 2024), with the Control condition set as the reference level. The model included condition (Control, Frequency, TP), ROI (left ROIs, right ROIs), and their interaction as fixed effects, as well as random intercepts for participants to account for within-subject variability. The significance of effects was evaluated using Type II Wald χ 2 tests via the Anova function from the car package (Fox & Weisberg, 2019). Planned pairwise comparisons were conducted using the emmeans package (Lenth & Piaskowski, 2026), with p-values adjusted for multiple comparisons using the Holm correction.

2.5.4 Sustained attention metric (lapse rates)

To measure individual differences in sustained attention, we computed lapse rates as the proportion of trials in which participants were in a poor attentional state (i.e., out-of-zone), following previous work (Debettencourt et al., 2018; Decker, Duncan, & Finn, 2023; Esterman et al., 2013; Fortenbaugh et al., 2018). We began by filtering the raw RT data obtained from our Sustained Attention Task, excluding trials with RTs shorter than 250 ms and longer than 1500 ms. We then detrended RTs separately within each participant and block. Specifically, for each participant × condition order × block order combination, we fit a linear model predicting RT from trial number within that block and extracted the residuals. These residuals were then re-centered by adding each participant's overall mean RT, thereby removing within-block linear drift (e.g., practice or fatigue effects) while preserving individual differences in overall response speed. Missing RTs were accounted for using moving-average interpolation based on the two preceding and two following trials. Finally, for each trial, we computed the absolute deviation of the interpolated, residualized RT from the mean interpolated residualized RT within each participant, block and condition. This triallevel deviance indexed attentional fluctuations. A five-trial Gaussian-weighted moving average smoothed the data. To establish a group-level threshold for attentional lapses, we first computed the median of this smoothed deviance for each participant and then averaged these medians across participants. Trials above this threshold were labeled as out-of-zone, while the trials below the threshold were classified as in-zone. Participants' lapse rates were operationalized as the proportion of out-of-zone trials out of the total number of trials (i.e., higher values reflected more time spent in a poor attentional state). These values were then used for correlating lapses with neural entrainment across participants. To validate the lapse index, we performed a linear mixed-effects model at the participant-by-condition level, treating participant as a random intercept. A Type II Wald χ 2 test revealed that overall lapse rates significantly predicted categorization accuracy, χ 2 (1) = 9.10, p = .003. In line with our predictions, higher lapse rates were associated with lower accuracy (b = -0.05, SE = 0.02, t = -3.02, p = .003).

2.5.5 Statistical analyses on lapse rates

We analyzed trial-level sustained-attention performance using generalized linear mixed models (GLMM) implemented in R (R Core Team, 2024) with the lme4 package (Bates et al., 2015). The dependent variable (zone) indicated whether each trial was categorized as in-zone or out-of-zone. The fixed-effects structure included condition, condition order, and block order, with a random intercept for participant. The factor condition (Control, Frequency, and TP) was coded with Control as the reference level. Significance of fixed effects was evaluated using Type II Wald χ 2 test via the Anova function from the car package (Fox & Weisberg, 2019). Planned contrasts were performed using the emmeans package (Lenth & Piaskowski, 2026) to compare the Control condition against the Frequency and TP conditions, as well as comparing Frequency and TP conditions against each other. Resulting p-values were adjusted for multiple comparisons using the Holm correction. Predicted probabilities and 95% confidence intervals were extracted from the model to generate effects plots using ggplot2 (Wickham, 2016).

2.5.6 Correlations between neural entrainment and lapse rates

To assess the relationship between sustained attention and neural entrainment, we fitted a linear mixed-effects model (LMM) predicting structure-rate entrainment (1 Hz) from lapse rate, condition, and their interaction, with a random intercept for participant. Structure-rate entrainment was quantified by averaging the BC sum of the target frequency (1 Hz) and its two higher harmonics (2 and 3 Hz) across the four exposure blocks within each condition. Planned contrasts of the interaction slopes were then performed using the emmeans package (Lenth & Piaskowski, 2026) to compare the Control condition with the Frequency and TP conditions, with p-values adjusted using the Holm correction.

2.5.7 Exploratory within-subject analysis of attentional state and neural entrainment

To test whether within-subject, trial-by-trial fluctuations in sustained attention were accompanied by corresponding changes in neural entrainment, we conducted a complementary analysis using Inter-Trial-Coherence (ITC), preregistered as exploratory, within the Frequency and TP conditions. This analysis was considered exploratory because attentional states were not experimentally imposed and because it required reconstructing in-zone and out-of-zone periods into continuous stretches containing complete pattern cycles. We used ITC rather than spectral power because the zeropadding required to preserve complete structure cycles maintains phase alignment but can alter amplitude estimates.

Starting from the interpolated and re-referenced data, EEG data were low-pass filtered at 30 Hz, to align with previous work (Batterink & Paller, 2017), and segmented into continuous stretches that remained entirely within the same attentional state, as determined from the behavioral lapse classification procedure. Stretches spanning different attentional states were excluded to avoid ambiguity in state assignment.

Within each stretch, data were aligned to the underlying four-syllable structure using syllable boundaries. Because some stretches began or ended mid-word, incomplete initial or final portions were zero-padded to preserve phase alignment. The resulting data were then split into in-zone and out-of-zone subsets, from which 6s epochs were extracted, corresponding to 24 syllables and six complete structure cycles. We verified that the in-zone and out-of-zone subsets were comparable in total duration and number of extracted epochs, despite minor differences in the number of contributing stretches and the proportion of zero-padding (see Supplementary Table 3 for details).

To compute ITC, the Fast Fourier transform (FFT) was first applied to each reconstructed in-zone and out-of-zone 6-s epoch. ITC was computed by extracting the complex Fourier coefficients for each epoch, normalizing each coefficient by its magnitude to obtain unit phase vectors, and then averaging the resulting phase vectors across epochs within each attentional state. To express observed ITC relative to a null distribution, we generated subject-and zone-specific surrogate distributions using phase-rotation time shifts. In each of 250 iterations, epochs were circularly shifted in time within ±700 ms via frequency-domain phase rotation, which preserves spectral properties while disrupting temporal alignment across epochs. Because surrogate distributions were computed separately for each participant and attentional state using the corresponding number of epochs, resulting z-scored ITC estimates were less susceptible to residual differences in data quantity across subsets. ITC was recomputed and observed ITC values were converted to z-scores using the corresponding surrogate mean and standard deviation. z-ITC values were averaged across predefined ROIs and summed across the 1-Hz structure frequency and its higher harmonics (2 Hz and 3 Hz). We then fit a linear mixed-effects model predicting z-ITC from condition, attentional zone, and their interaction, with random intercepts for participants.

2.5.8 Familiarity task

We analyzed participants' familiarity ratings using linear mixed-effects models (LMMs), separately for the Frequency and TP conditions. For each condition, familiarity ratings were modeled as a function of string type (correct, partially wrong, completely wrong) and repetition number (1-3) as fixed effects, with a random intercept for participant. Post-hoc pairwise comparisons between string types were conducted using the emmeans package (Lenth & Piaskowski, 2026) with pairwise contrasts to examine differences in familiarity ratings.

2.5.9 Syllable sorting task

In the Frequency condition, accuracy was calculated as the mean proportion of syllables correctly classified as frequent or infrequent. Accuracy scores were averaged across participants, and a one-sample t-test was conducted to compare performance against the 50% chance level.

In the TP condition, accuracy reflects the proportion of adjacent within-unit transitions preserved in the correct relative order. For each four-syllable unit, transitions between consecutive syllables (e.g., 1-2, 2-3, and 3-4) were scored as correct only when the syllables were placed in the same box and in adjacent positions in the correct order. Transition scores were averaged across units to obtain a mean TP accuracy score for each participant. Due to the lack of an analytical chance level, chance performance was estimated via 1000 simulations of random syllable arrangements that respected the constraints of the sorting task (i.e., each of the 16 syllables was randomly assigned to one of the 16 available slots, with exactly four syllables per box), yielding a chance benchmark of 5.01%. Participants' mean TP accuracy was compared against this simulated chance level using a one-sample t-test.

3 Results

3.1 Neural entrainment to structure

Neural entrainment at the syllable rate reflected low-level synchronization to the periodic input and was present in all conditions (all ps < .001; see Supplementary Table 1 for full statistics). By contrast, neural entrainment at the structure rate reflected sensitivity to the statistical structure embedded in the syllable sequences (Fig. 2a). As expected, the Control condition showed no evidence of entrainment at this frequency (both ps > .05), whereas both structured conditions (Frequency and TP) showed reliable entrainment (all ps < .001; see Supplementary Table 2 and Supplementary Fig. 1). A Type II Wald χ 2 test confirmed a main effect of condition on structure-rate entrainment, χ 2 (2) = 199.78, p < .001, and no main effect of ROI (left vs. right occipito-temporal hemisphere), χ 2 (1) = 0.08, p = .784, nor their interaction, χ 2 (2) = 1.20, p = .549. Relative to the Control condition, entrainment was stronger in both the Frequency (b = 0.04, SE = 0.005, t = 7.13, p < .001) and the TP condition (b = 0.07, SE = 0.005, t = 14.13, p < .001). Neural entrainment was also significantly stronger in the TP condition than in the Frequency condition (b = 0.04, SE = 0.005, t = 7.00, p < .001).

3.2 Fewer attentional lapses when structure is present

As shown in Fig. 2b, a Type II Wald χ 2 test revealed a significant main effect of condition on the proportion of out-of-zone trials, χ 2 (2) = 79.45, p < .001. Relative to the Control condition, both the Frequency (b = -0.04, SE = 0.02, z = -2.34, p = .020) and the TP (b = -0.13, SE = 0.02, z = -8.62, p < .001) conditions were associated with significantly fewer out-of-zone trials. Furthermore, the TP condition showed a marked reduction in lapses compared to the Frequency condition (b = -0.10, SE = 0.02, z = -6.29, p < .001). These results indicate that participants experienced fewer attentional lapses when statistical structure was present, particularly in the TP condition. As expected given previous work (Esterman et al., 2013), additional main effects of condition order, χ 2 (1) = 125.47, p < .001, and block order, χ 2 (1) = 53.34, p < .001, indicated that the proportion of out-of-zone trials increased both across conditions and within conditions as the experiment progressed.

3.3 Attentional lapses covary with neural entrainment across participants

A Type II Wald χ 2 test revealed a significant interaction between condition and lapse rate (i.e., the proportion of trials spent in a poor attentional state out of the total number of trials) on neural entrainment amplitude, χ 2 (2) = 7.09, p = .029. Planned contrasts of the interaction slopes revealed that, relative to the Control condition, higher lapse rates were associated with significantly stronger reductions in neural entrainment in both the Frequency condition (b = -0.07, p = .033) and the TP condition (b = -0.06, p = .034). Fig. 2c shows within-condition Spearman correlations for visualization purposes, whereas the p-values reported here derive from the regression model.

3.4 Attentional lapses covary with moment-by-moment neural entrainment within participant

We directly tested whether within-subject fluctuations in sustained attention were accompanied by corresponding changes in neural entrainment. A Type II Wald χ 2 test on z-ITC revealed significant main effects of attentional zone, χ 2 (1) = 9.25, p = .002, and condition, χ 2 (1) = 42.39, p < .001, indicating greater neural entrainment during in-zone than out-of-zone periods (Fig. 2d) and higher entrainment in the TP than in the Frequency condition. The interaction between zone (in-vs. out-zone) and condition was not significant, χ 2 (1) = 2.13, p = .144. These findings extend the between-participant results by showing that not only do individuals with fewer attentional lapses exhibit higher overall neural entrainment, but trial-by-trial fluctuations in attention within a given individual also covary with corresponding changes in neural tracking.

3.5 Behavioral evidence for learning structured input

To assess learning, we used two complementary measures: familiarity ratings and a syllable sorting task. Considering the Frequency condition first, familiarity ratings (Fig. 3a) showed only a marginal effect of string type (correct, partially wrong, completely wrong), χ 2 (2) = 5.10, p = .078. Consistent with this weak overall effect, ratings did not differ reliably between correct and entirely wrong strings (b = 0.05, SE = 0.03, t = 1.72, p = .199), nor between correct and partially wrong strings (b = -0.01, SE = 0.03, t = -0.41, p = .910). However, sorting accuracy (M = 0.55, SD = 0.15; Fig. 3b) was significantly above chance (0.50), t = 2.59, p = .011, d = 0.29, 95% CI [0.51, 0.58], indicating reliable discrimination of frequency-based structure beyond initial exposure on one of two measures.

In the TP condition, familiarity ratings showed a marginal effect of string type (correct, partially wrong, completely wrong), χ 2 (2) = 5.70, p = .058. Post-hoc comparisons revealed that ratings were higher for correct than completely wrong strings (b = 0.07, SE = 0.03, t = 2.35, p = .049). By contrast, ratings for partially wrong strings did not differ from correct items (b = 0.05, SE = 0.03, t = 1.53, p = .275), suggesting that participants did not reliably distinguish between fully and partially structured strings. Correlations between familiarity ratings, neural entrainment, and attention at the participant level are shown in Supplementary Fig. 2 3 4 5. Sorting accuracy (M = 0.09, SD = 0.10; Fig. 3b) was above chance (0.05), t = 3.63, p < .001, d = 0.41, 95% CI [0.07, 0.11], further indicating that participants gained at least some explicit knowledge of the TP structure.

4 Discussion

The ability to track regularities in the environment is a fundamental feature of human cognition, shaping representations and processing across domains including language (Batterink & Paller, 2017 ), vision (De Rosa et al., 2022), and music (Van Hedger et al., 2022). In this work, we investigated its internal dynamics by testing whether the brain's sensitivity to structure varies alongside naturally occurring fluctuations in one's attentional state. We found that lapses in attention are associated with concurrent reductions in neural entrainment to the regularities. This was observed both between individuals-that is, people with more lapses tend to show reduced entrainment overall-and within individuals-manifested as enhanced entrainment during periods of heightened attentional focus. These findings speak directly to a central question in the field: whether attention matters for statistical learning. It matters greatly. Indeed, our within-subject analysis reveals that neural entrainment is reduced when individuals are in a worse attentional state, providing strong evidence that attention dynamically interacts with the online processing of statistical regularities. This is very much in line with other work showing that one's attentional state shapes learning outcomes more generally (Cowan et al., 2024).

The present findings also suggest that the relationship between statistical learning and attention may be reciprocal; while heightened attentional focus is accompanied by enhanced entrainment, the presence of statistical regularities concurrently helps capture and stabilize focus, consistent with evidence that attention is spontaneously biased toward structure (Zhao et al., 2013). This interpretation is specifically supported by our finding that attentional engagement was significantly higher during the presentation of structured versus random visual streams. Within this framework, neural entrainment does not merely reflect a passive downstream effect of attention; instead, it may index a dynamic interplay in which sensitivity to structure and attentional state covary over time. This aligns with previous research showing that attention itself is not static, but appears to fluctuate in response to the richness, predictability, or entropy of the input (Forest et al., 2022).

If attentional engagement depends on the predictability of the input, it follows that different types of statistical structures may influence attentional states and neural entrainment in distinct ways. Indeed, this work also shows that both attentional states and neural entrainment varied based on the type of structure that was being presented: they were greater for TP-based than frequency-based regularities. This is noteworthy since the statistical learning literature has focused for years on the former (Frost et al., 2019), building on the original paradigm introduced by Saffran and colleagues (Saffran et al., 1996). However, our perceptual input is very rich and includes regularities of many different sorts. Recently, one of these regularities, that is, item frequency, has attracted some attention (De Rosa et al., 2022; Growns et al., 2025; Zhou et al., 2020). This considerably extends the remit of statistical learning and its potential role in other cognitive functions; for example, word frequency was suggested to be at the basis of the distinction between grammatical classes, one of the fundamental ingredients of proficient language use (Mintz, 2003; Reeder et al., 2013). Interestingly, TP-based and frequency-based regularities also differ in the level at which prediction operates. In our Frequency condition, participants could anticipate the likely occurrence of a syllable category (frequent vs. infrequent) but had no basis for predicting which specific item would appear next. By contrast, in our TP condition, each element strongly constrained the identity of the following one, supporting fine-grained, item-level predictions. Overall, frequency-based and TP-based statistics may support different computational goals within statistical learning, yielding broad sensitivity to environmental regularities on the one hand, and precise internal models of sequential structure on the other.

Crucially, however, the impact of attentional lapses on neural entrainment to structured input was comparable across structured conditions and in both our betweenand within-participants analyses. This convergence suggests that while the brain may prioritize and encode these structures via different predictive levels, the link between sustained attention and neural tracking may reflect a constraint that operates independently of the type of statistical structure. These results point to a robust coupling between attentional states and the extraction of statistical regularities and highlight the need for future work to determine the principles that govern which types of statistical structures are most readily encoded and tracked by the brain.

As a final note, we highlight the contrast between the very strong entrainment that we observed here and the scattered signs of behavioral learning, which were quite weak and only emerged in some tasks and contrasts. This result echoes prior differences that have been shown between neural entrainment and behavior (Benjamin et al., 2025; Herrera-Chaves et al., 2026; Moser et al., 2021), and suggests that this type of statistical learning may produce memory representations that are relatively fleeting or not easily accessible, at least under typical exposure conditions. This resonates with an articulated view of the neural and cognitive mechanisms that lie behind statistical learning (Batterink & Paller, 2017; Thiessen et al., 2013) and invites further research to understand how transient neural tracking transitions into accessible behavioral knowledge.

Together, these findings show that the human brain is highly sensitive to statistical structure, capable of detecting different types of regularities even under rapid and continuous input. At the same time, this sensitivity appears to be tempered by one's attentional state, contrary to more simplistic views of statistical learning as an encapsulated, attention-independent mechanism that automatically translates regularities into new memory representations (Batterink & Paller, 2019; Duncan & Theeuwes, 2020): neural tracking is shaped by the type of regularity presented and covaries with fluctuations in attentional engagement. This early index of statistical learning, therefore, appears to be the result of a dynamic interplay between perceptual and attentional mechanisms.

Finn). 1 Relationship between online (structure-rate neural entrainment) and offline (familiarity scores) measures of statistical learning

1.1 Analysis

To assess the relationship between structure-rate neural entrainment (1 Hz) and offline behavioral learning, as assessed via the familiarity judgment task, we fit linear models predicting two different familiarity scores from neural entrainment amplitude, condition (Frequency vs. TP), and their interaction. Specifically, we first focused on the traditionally used familiarity score, calculated as the mean of correct (C) minus completely wrong (CW) syllable strings. We then looked at a different learning score that reflects participants' discrimination accuracy between correct and partially wrong (PW) syllable strings. Significance of effects was evaluated using Type II F -tests implemented via the Anova function from the car package.

1.2 Results

For the contrast between correct and completely wrong items, the linear model revealed no main effect of structure-rate neural entrainment, F (1, 152) = 1.34, p = .249, and no main effect of condition, F (1, 152) = 0.006, p = .940. The interaction between structure-rate neural entrainment and condition was significant, F (1, 152) = 4.72, p = .031. Follow-up Pearson correlations indicated that higher neural entrainment at 1 Hz was associated with higher familiarity scores in the Frequency condition (r = 0.24, p = .036), but not in the TP condition (r = -0.12, p = .294).

Considering the contrast between correct and partially wrong strings, the model revealed a significant effect of structure-rate neural entrainment, F (1, 152) = 6.57, p = .011, no main effect of condition, F (1, 152) = 0.12, p = .725, and no significant interaction between neural entrainment and condition, F (1, 152) = 0.001, p = .895. 2.1 Analysis

Using the same approach, we next examined the relationship between attentional state and offline behavioral learning as assessed by the familiarity judgment task. As before, we fit linear models predicting two different familiarity scores (i.e., C -CW and C -PW) from lapse rate, condition (Frequency vs. TP), and their interaction. Significance of effects was evaluated using Type II F -tests via the Anova function from the car package.

3.1 Position accuracy in the TP Sorting Task

We investigated whether participants were able to group syllables solely on the basis of their absolute position within a sequence. For each participant, we computed Position Accuracy as the proportion of syllables correctly placed in their exact target position, irrespective of correct syllable grouping. Because each syllable had four possible position slots, chance performance was 25%. To test whether participants tracked positional information, we conducted a one-sample ttest comparing mean observed position accuracy against the 25% chance baseline. Participants correctly placed syllables in their target position 31.33% of the time (SD = 14.30%), performing significantly above chance, t(77) = 3.91, p < .001, d = 0.44. This indicates that participants successfully sorted syllables according to their absolute ordinal position.

3.2 Attentional state is not influenced by the position of the colored syllables within the streams

We investigated whether position of colored syllables (1-4) within a sequence influenced participants' attentional states. To test this, we fit a generalized linear mixed-effects model (GLMM) with a binomial distribution, predicting Zone (in-zone vs. out-of-zone). The model included Condition (Control, Frequency, TP), Syllable Position (treated as a 4-level categorical factor: 1, 2, 3, 4), and their interaction as fixed effects. Participant was included as a random intercept to account for repeated measures. A Type II Wald χ 2 test revealed, as expected, a significant main effect of Condition, χ 2 (2) = 77.86, p < .001. Crucially, we observed no significant main effect of Syllable Position, χ 2 (3) = 1.81, p = .612, nor was there a significant interaction between Condition and Syllable Position, χ 2 (6) = 6.73, p = .346. These results indicate that participants' attentional state was not systematically influenced by the colored syllable's ordinal position, nor did the effect of position vary by condition.

In the Frequency condition (blue and orange; second line of the sinusoid and second line on the bottom panel), infrequent syllables (orange) are embedded periodically, with one infrequent syllable occurring every fourth position among frequent syllables (blue).
